1	---

7	
8	# Dream Review Report — Full Mode
9	
10	**Paper:** "Multi-Class Annotation Errors Are Not Created Equal: Bias Taxonomy, Sensitivity Analysis, and Correction Guidance for LLM-Annotated Causal Studies"
11	
12	**Review mode:** Full (2 Reviewers + Roundtable)
13	**Date:** 2026-05-20
14	
15	---
16	
17	## Reviewer Assignments
18	
19	| Reviewer | Focus | Score | Confidence |
20	|----------|-------|-------|------------|
21	| R1 | Methods + Experiments | 3 (Weak Reject) | 4/5 |
22	| R2 | Novelty + Related Work | 4 (Weak Accept) | 4/5 |
23	
24	---
25	
26	## Step 1: Independent Reviews
27	
28	### R1 — Methods & Experiments
29	
30	**Summary:** The paper proposes a systematic framework for analyzing multi-class (K≥3) annotation error in LLM-annotated causal studies, covering bias taxonomy, sensitivity analysis (ASV), and correction guidance across six DAG topologies. The ambition is commendable but execution shows significant gaps in theoretical coverage, empirical validation, and practical demonstration.
31	
32	**Strengths:**
33	- Well-motivated problem: the gap between binary measurement error theory and multi-class CSS practice is real and under-addressed
34	- The exposure reversal finding (85% of random K=3 confusion matrices produce direction reversal) is a strong, quantified result with clear practical implications
35	- ASV concept is a natural and well-designed analogy to E-values, with clean formal proof for the confounding topology
36	- Ambitious scope covering 6 topologies, general K extensions, empirical validation, correction framework, and open-source toolkit — rare to see this complete a pipeline
37	
38	**Weaknesses:**
39	
40	- **(Major) Theory-MC match rate of 76% undermines the taxonomy's generality.** 10 out of 42 topology-structure combinations fail to match. Front-door is worst at 3/6. The paper honestly reports this but the remedy — treating front-door expressions as "MC-validated conjectures rather than exact theorems" — weakens a core contribution. The mismatch pattern (concentrated in complex topologies with extreme C structures) suggests the first-order approximations break down precisely where practitioners need guidance most.
41	
42	- **(Major) ASV formal properties are heterogeneous and incompletely established.** Monotonicity fails for collider (7%) and front-door (3%). Sharpness is formally proved only for confounding. Aggregate 95% coverage is 0.85 — systematically below nominal. The collider non-monotonicity mechanism (moderate misclassification opens collider path, larger misclassification dilutes it) is interesting but means ASV cannot be trusted as a monotone sensitivity measure for this topology. The paper recommends using 99% coverage level as conservative choice, but this is a patch, not a fix.
43	
44	- **(Major) Empirical validation is entirely in the "safe regime."** All 9 empirical confusion matrices are diagonal-dominant (dominance 0.74–0.87). No near-permutation structures, no direction reversals, no ASV fragility observed in practice. 5/9 ASV values are trivially large (>50). The 4 meaningful ASV cases provide correct concordance but N=4 is too small for statistical claims. The paper's most dramatic theoretical finding (exposure reversal) has zero empirical demonstration. This is a theory-practice gap that undermines the paper's practical narrative.
45	
46	- **(Major) Correction framework provides only 13 percentage points above trivial baseline.** 78% accuracy vs 65% "always recommend MLA." The decision tree depth-4 with leaf purity 0.73 suggests the framework struggles to capture the topology-structure interactions it claims to map. MLA dominates 65% of recommendations. The framework's main value is in the 35% exposure scenarios where it recommends gold labels — but this is essentially a single rule ("if exposure topology with near-permutation C, collect gold labels"), not a framework.
47	
48	- **(Minor) Binarization analysis is incomplete.** K=5,7 results are only reported for confounding; the other 5 topologies lack higher-K binarization data. Max delta 0.091 is described as "real but modest" — reviewers may question whether a 0.09 bias discrepancy is practically material when CSS effect sizes are 0.1–0.3. No direction reversal from binarization further weakens this as a contribution.
49	
50	- **(Minor) No figures in the paper.** The paper is entirely tables and text. For a paper whose core message is a "sharp dichotomy" and spectral analysis, the absence of heatmaps, sensitivity curves, eigenvalue spectra, or confusion matrix visualizations is a major presentation deficit. Figures would dramatically improve communication of the exposure reversal mechanism, the theory-MC gap pattern, and the ASV calibration results.
51	
52	**Questions for Authors:**
53	1. Can you provide the front-door bias expressions explicitly so the community can identify the approximation source? At 3/6 match, "conjecture" status demands transparency about what's conjectured.
54	2. For the collider 7% monotonicity violations: is δ∈[0.3, 0.5] the only violation range? Can you characterize the full failure surface?
55	3. Why not include semi-synthetic experiments (injecting controlled degradation into empirical C) to bridge the theory-practice gap? This seems like an obvious experiment.
56	4. The correction framework's 12% "no existing method adequate" scenarios — are these characterized enough to guide methodological development?
57	
58	**Missing Experiments/Analyses:**
59	- Semi-synthetic stress tests: degrade empirical confusion matrices to demonstrate ASV transitioning from robust to fragile
60	- DGP robustness: vary structural equation forms (nonlinear, interaction terms) beyond the linear assumption
```

Front-door is worst at 3/6. The paper honestly reports this but the remedy — treating front-door expressions as \"MC-validated conjectures rather than exact theorems\" — weakens a core contribution. The mismatch pattern (concentrated in complex topologies with extreme C structures) suggests the first-order approximations break down precisely where practitioners need guidance most.\n\n- **(Major) ASV formal properties are heterogeneous and incompletely established.** Monotonicity fails for collider (7%) and front-door (3%). Sharpness is formally proved only for confounding. Aggregate 95% coverage is 0.85 — systematically below nominal. The collider non-monotonicity mechanism (moderate misclassification opens collider path, larger misclassification dilutes it) is interesting but means ASV cannot be trusted as a monotone sensitivity measure for this topology. The paper recommends using 99% coverage level as conservative choice, but this is a patch, not a fix.\n\n- **(Major) Empirical validation is entirely in the \"safe regime.\"** All 9 empirical confusion matrices are diagonal-dominant (dominance 0.74–0.87). No near-permutation structures, no direction reversals, no ASV fragility observed in practice. 5/9 ASV values are trivially large (>50). The 4 meaningful ASV cases provide correct concordance but N=4 is too small for statistical claims. The paper's most dramatic theoretical finding (exposure reversal) has zero empirical demonstration. This is a theory-practice gap that undermines the paper's practical narrative.\n\n- **(Major) Correction framework provides only 13 percentage points above trivial baseline.** 78% accuracy vs 65% \"always recommend MLA.\" The decision tree depth-4 with leaf purity 0.73 suggests the framework struggles to capture the topology-structure interactions it claims to map. MLA dominates 65% of recommendations. The framework's main value is in the 35% exposure scenarios where it recommends gold labels — but this is essentially a single rule (\"if exposure topology with near-permutation C, collect gold labels\"), not a framework.\n\n- **(Minor) Binarization analysis is incomplete.** K=5,7 results are only reported for confounding; the other 5 topologies lack higher-K binarization data. Max delta 0.091 is described as \"real but modest\" — reviewers may question whether a 0.09 bias discrepancy is practically material when CSS effect sizes are 0.1–0.3. No direction reversal from binarization further weakens this as a contribution.\n\n- **(Minor) No figures in the paper.** The paper is entirely tables and text. For a paper whose core message is a \"sharp dichotomy\" and spectral analysis, the absence of heatmaps, sensitivity curves, eigenvalue spectra, or confusion matrix visualizations is a major presentation deficit. Figures would dramatically improve communication of the exposure reversal mechanism, the theory-MC gap pattern, and the ASV calibration results.\n\n**Questions for Authors:**\n1. Can you provide the front-door bias expressions explicitly so the community can identify the approximation source? At 3/6 match, \"conjecture\" status demands transparency about what's conjectured.\n2. For the collider 7% monotonicity violations: is δ∈[0.3, 0.5] the only violation range? Can you characterize the full failure surface?\n3. Why not include semi-synthetic experiments (injecting controlled degradation into empirical C) to bridge the theory-practice gap? This seems like an obvious experiment.\n4. The correction framework's 12% \"no existing method adequate\" scenarios — are these characterized enough to guide methodological development?\n\n**Missing Experiments/Analyses:**\n- Semi-synthetic stress tests: degrade empirical confusion matrices to demonstrate ASV transitioning from robust to fragile\n- DGP robustness: vary structural equation forms (nonlinear, interaction terms) beyond the linear assumption",
      "numLines": 60,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 228
    }

```text
60	- DGP robustness: vary structural equation forms (nonlinear, interaction terms) beyond the linear assumption
61	- Multiple binarization folding schemes (not just one collapse strategy)
62	- At least one high-K (K≥5) low-accuracy empirical task to demonstrate non-trivial ASV behavior
63	- Power analysis for MC validation: is N=10,000 sufficient for K=7 (49 cells)?
64	
65	---
66	
67	### R2 — Novelty & Related Work
68	
69	**Summary:** The paper bridges measurement error theory from epidemiology with the practical needs of CSS researchers using LLM annotations. The topology-dependent direction reversal dichotomy is a genuinely novel organizing result, and the theory-to-practice pipeline is admirably complete. However, related work is critically thin, key contradictions with prior literature are unaddressed, and formal proofs are claimed but not provided.
70	
71	**Strengths:**
72	- **The topology-dependent dichotomy is genuinely novel.** No prior work has systematically characterized direction reversal risk as a function of the annotated variable's role in the DAG. Brenner (1993) proved polytomous misclassification *can* bias in either direction but did not map this to DAG topology. Hernán & Cole (2009) provided the conceptual framework (causal diagrams for measurement bias) but without K×K confusion matrix formalism. This paper's contribution is the synthesis: which topologies, under what C structures, with what severity.
73	
74	- **The complete pipeline (taxonomy → ASV → correction → toolkit) is rare.** Most papers in this space deliver one piece. The end-to-end coverage, with an installable toolkit, makes this practically useful even if individual components have limitations.
75	
76	- **The binarization analysis strategically preempts the obvious objection** ("why not just collapse to binary and use existing theory?"). Showing that binarization loses magnitude information, even if it preserves direction, is necessary groundwork.
77	
78	- **Honest limitation reporting.** The paper is notably transparent about theory-MC gaps, ASV coverage shortfalls, collider non-monotonicity, and the diagonal-dominant empirical regime. This intellectual honesty is appreciated.
79	
80	**Weaknesses:**
81	
82	- **(Major) Related work is critically thin — approximately 10 references for a paper bridging three fields.** A paper at this intersection should cite 25–30 works. Critical omissions:
83	  - **Carroll et al. (2006)** — "Measurement Error in Nonlinear Models" is the standard textbook; the matrix-form bias expressions likely relate to results there.
84	  - **Lash et al. (2009/2014/2021)** — "Applying Quantitative Bias Analysis" is the practitioner's guide to exactly the kind of sensitivity analysis this paper proposes; ASV must be positioned against this tradition.
85	  - **Ogburn & VanderWeele (2012)** — bias formulas for misclassified mediators/confounders in specific DAGs; direct predecessor for the taxonomy.
86	  - **Dosemeci et al. (1990)** — polytomous exposure misclassification bias in case-control studies; directly relevant to the exposure reversal finding.
87	  - **Angelopoulos et al. (2023)** — PPI/PPI++ is explicitly recommended by the correction framework but never cited.
88	  - **Grimmer et al. (2022)** — "Text as Data" is the standard reference for annotation quality in CSS.
89	  - The paper recommends DSL (Design-based Sensitivity Limits) without citing the source.
90	  
91	  This is not cosmetic — without proper positioning, the reader cannot assess what is truly new vs. what is known but scattered across epidemiology and statistics literatures.
92	
93	- **(Major) The Brenner (1993) contradiction is unreconciled.** Brenner proved that non-differential misclassification of a polytomous *confounder* can bias in either direction. Yet the paper's confounding topology shows 0% reversal rate with 6/6 theory-MC match. The likely explanation is an estimand difference (Brenner may use a different adjustment strategy or effect measure), but this is never discussed. A reader familiar with Brenner would immediately question the confounding result, and the paper provides no help.
94	
95	- **(Major) Formal proofs claimed but no proof appendix provided.** ASV monotonicity and sharpness proofs for confounding are described as "formal" contributions, yet no appendix or supplementary material contains the actual proofs. For a paper whose contribution is partly theoretical, this is a serious omission. Reviewers cannot verify correctness.
96	
97	- **(Minor) Impact scope may be narrower than claimed.** The paper targets CSS researchers using LLM annotations for causal inference — a real but relatively small community. The theoretical contributions (bias taxonomy, ASV) are general, but the empirical validation and framing are CSS-specific. The exposure reversal finding is most relevant to researchers who LLM-annotate the *treatment variable itself*, which may be a minority of use cases (many CSS studies annotate covariates or outcomes, not treatments).
98	
99	- **(Minor) The "first systematic characterization" claim needs qualification.** Hernán & Cole (2009) provided systematic causal-diagram reasoning about measurement error. The novelty is in the K×K matrix formalization and the quantitative taxonomy, not in the idea of connecting DAG structure to measurement error consequences.
100	
101	**Questions for Authors:**
102	1. How do you reconcile your confounding result (0% reversal, monotone toward OVB) with Brenner (1993)'s finding that polytomous confounder misclassification can bias in either direction? Is the difference in the estimand (e.g., risk ratio vs. risk difference), the adjustment method, or the non-differential assumption?
103	2. Will proof appendices be provided in a camera-ready version? If so, can you summarize the proof strategy for confounding monotonicity?
104	3. What fraction of published CSS causal studies use LLM-annotated exposure variables (vs. confounders, mediators, outcomes)? This would help calibrate the practical importance of the exposure reversal finding.
105	4. Why are PPI and DSL recommended by the correction framework but their source papers not cited?
106	
107	**Missing References / Positioning Issues:**
108	- Carroll et al. (2006) — foundational measurement error textbook
109	- Lash et al. (2009/2014/2021) — quantitative bias analysis practitioner guide
110	- Ogburn & VanderWeele (2012) — bias formulas for misclassified confounders/mediators
111	- Dosemeci et al. (1990) — polytomous exposure misclassification
112	- Veierød & Laake (2001) — polychotomous confounder misclassification
113	- Angelopoulos et al. (2023) — PPI/PPI++ (recommended but uncited)
114	- Grimmer et al. (2022) — "Text as Data"
115	- Egami et al. (2024) — LLMs as measurement tools in social science
116	- Source paper for DSL (recommended but uncited)
117	
118	---
119	
120	## Step 2: Cross-Sharing — Key Convergences and Divergences
121	
122	**Convergences (both reviewers agree):**
123	1. The core contribution — topology-dependent dichotomy for multi-class annotation error — is genuinely novel and practically relevant
124	2. Empirical validation is the weakest component: diagonal-dominant confusion matrices prevent demonstration of the most interesting theoretical predictions
125	3. The complete pipeline (taxonomy → ASV → correction → toolkit) is a strength
126	4. The paper is notably honest about its limitations
127	5. ASV collider non-monotonicity (7%) and front-door instability (3%) are real concerns for the sensitivity analysis contribution
128	
129	**Divergences:**
130	- **R1 weighs the theory-MC gap (76%) more heavily** as a methodological flaw; **R2 views it as honest but manageable** if properly positioned against prior work
131	- **R2 identifies critical related work gaps** that R1 did not flag — the ~10 references are far too few for a cross-disciplinary paper, and the Brenner contradiction is a red flag for epidemiology-literate reviewers
132	- **R1 is more concerned about the correction framework's marginal value** (13pp above trivial baseline); **R2 sees the framework as valuable for its completeness** and open problem identification even if accuracy is modest
133	- **Overall assessment:** R1 sees accumulated methodological shortcomings outweighing the novelty; R2 sees the novelty as sufficient to warrant acceptance if presentation issues are fixed
134	
135	## Step 3: Convergence Discussion
136	
137	After cross-examination, the reviewers converge on the following assessment:
138	
139	**The paper tackles a genuinely important and under-addressed problem.** The topology-dependent dichotomy is novel, the pipeline is complete, and the practical motivation is strong. These are not in dispute.
```

_source line 3303 · 2026-05-21T05:13:10.412Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019SDw1KKeojubjyuWywfcFB`</summary>

```text
1	---
2	dream_id: dream_20260520T074549
3	tier: best
4	overall_score: 4 - Weak Accept
5	timestamp: 2026-05-20T08:52:14.057210+00:00
6	---
7	
8	## R1 Review: Methods and Experiments
9	
10	### Summary
11	This paper presents a systematic framework for analyzing how multi-class (K>=3) annotation errors propagate bias in causal inference across six canonical DAG topologies. The core finding -- that the classical "toward-null" guarantee for non-differential misclassification breaks in a topology-dependent manner -- is validated through closed-form derivations, Monte Carlo simulation (100K random confusion matrices per topology), and empirical confusion matrices from 3 LLMs on 3 CSS tasks (~270K annotations). The three contributions (bias taxonomy, ASV sensitivity framework, correction guidance) form a coherent theory-to-practice pipeline. The experimental execution is comprehensive, but several methodological concerns warrant attention.
12	
13	### Strengths
14	
15	S1. **Rigorous MC validation design with appropriate controls.** The theory-MC validation uses a well-designed matrix of 6 topologies x 6 C structures x 1000 reps x N=10,000, achieving 35/36 match within 95% CIs. The single mismatch (front-door + rank-deficient C, delta=0.003) is transparently reported and shown to resolve at N=50,000 -- this is exactly the right way to handle boundary cases. The K=2 consistency checks across all topologies anchor the multi-class results to classical theory, making the departure at K>=3 convincing.
16	
17	S2. **The 100K random C search is well-powered for violation rate estimation.** With 100K draws, the standard error on a violation rate of 0.85 (exposure) is ~0.001, and even for the lowest non-zero rate (front-door, 0.34) the SE is ~0.0015. The violation rates are estimated with high precision. The zero-violation topologies (confounding, instrument, M-bias) at 100K draws give strong evidence of true monotonicity preservation (upper 95% bound on true violation rate < 0.00003).
18	
19	S3. **Honest scope management on proofs.** The separation of formally proved (confounding, mediation, collider) vs. MC-validated conjectures (M-bias, instrument, front-door) for ASV sharpness is intellectually honest and appropriately scoped. The numerical sharpness gaps (<0.008) for the conjectured topologies are convincing supporting evidence. The three different proof techniques (algebraic/spectral gap, envelope theorem, Lagrangian/KKT) demonstrate genuine mathematical engagement rather than a single trick applied mechanically.
20	
21	S4. **Comprehensive correction framework validation.** The 432-scenario grid (6 topologies x 6 C structures x 3 K values x 4 effect sizes) with 95% recommendation accuracy is thorough. The transparent failure analysis -- 14 near-ties (MLA ~= PPI within 0.005) and 7 genuine failures under extreme C + small N -- is more informative than a single headline accuracy number. The "no adequate method" designation for 15% of scenarios is scientifically honest.
22	
23	S5. **Dirichlet subspace analysis grounds the theoretical results in practical LLM accuracy ranges.** The stratified analysis (diag>=0.5, 0.7, 0.9) showing that sign reversal vanishes at high accuracy but amplification persists at ~12% regardless of accuracy is a nuanced finding that prevents both over- and under-interpretation of the headline 85% violation rate.
24	
25	### Weaknesses
26	
27	W1. **[Major] The non-differential misclassification assumption is load-bearing but insufficiently stress-tested.** The entire theoretical framework rests on P(V_tilde | V, other variables) = P(V_tilde | V). However, LLM annotation errors are known to depend on text features, context length, and semantic difficulty -- variables that likely correlate with other DAG variables. The paper's own finding that prompt variants shift C by Frobenius distance 0.04-0.12 suggests that the error process is sensitive to conditioning context. No experiment tests what happens to the reversal rates under mild violations of non-differentiality (e.g., strata-specific C matrices with 10-20% variation). Without this robustness check, the practical applicability of the framework to real LLM annotation pipelines is uncertain. This is not a theoretical concern that can be deferred to future work -- it is central to whether the framework is useful for its stated target audience (CSS researchers using LLMs).
28	
29	W2. **[Major] The empirical "topological contexts" are likely artificial.** The paper applies each CSS task (protest, stance, toxicity) to three topological contexts (mediation, collider, confounding/instrument) for ASV calibration (Table 11, 9 scenarios). But these tasks have inherent causal structures -- protest event coding is typically used as an exposure or outcome variable in conflict studies, not as a mediator or collider. If the topological assignments are hypothetical rather than drawn from real published CSS research designs, the 8/9 ASV concordance result validates the framework's internal consistency but not its real-world utility. The paper should either (a) cite specific published CSS studies that use these tasks in these topological roles, or (b) explicitly acknowledge this as a mechanics demonstration. As written, the empirical validation overstates its ecological validity.
30	
31	W3. **[Major] ASV coverage at 95% nominal = 0.907 (paper summary) or 0.952 (best-tier paper) -- which is it?** The task description states average 95%-nominal coverage = 0.907, while the paper's Table 6 reports 95.2%. If the actual coverage is 0.907, this is a 4.3 percentage point shortfall that undermines the ASV's calibration claim. The held-out empirical coverage is 0.94 (Table 12 text). These numbers need reconciliation. Coverage below 0.93 at 95% nominal would be a serious concern -- it means the ASV bound is anti-conservative (under-covers) more than 2 percentage points beyond the nominal level, potentially giving false reassurance. The paper should report coverage across different regimes (high vs. low diagonal dominance, reversal-prone vs. monotonic topologies) to identify where the shortfall concentrates.
32	
33	W4. **[Moderate] The 100K random C search uses uniform Dirichlet sampling, which overweights pathological matrices.** The headline violation rates (exposure 85%, mediation 52%, etc.) are computed over uniformly sampled row-stochastic matrices. But real LLM confusion matrices cluster in the high-diagonal-dominance region (diag >= 0.6-0.9, as shown in Table 10). The Dirichlet subspace analysis partially addresses this for exposure but is not reported for mediation, collider, or front-door topologies. The paper should report violation rates stratified by diagonal dominance for ALL reversal-prone topologies, not just exposure. Without this, the headline rates may be misleading -- if mediation's 52% drops to 5% at diag>=0.7, the practical risk is much lower than implied.
34	
35	W5. **[Moderate] MC replications for ASV validation may be underpowered.** The ASV validation uses 1000 scenarios x 500 reps per scenario (plan_004). For coverage estimation at the 95% level, with 1000 scenarios the standard error on coverage is sqrt(0.95*0.05/1000) = 0.0069, meaning the 95% CI on coverage is approximately +/- 1.4 percentage points. This is sufficient to distinguish 0.95 from 0.90 but not from 0.93. The per-topology coverage numbers (mediation 94.9%, collider 94.8%) are within the noise band of the 95% nominal. Increasing to 5000-10000 scenarios would provide a sharper test of calibration.
36	
37	W6. **[Moderate] The correction framework's "accuracy" metric is not well-defined.** The paper says the framework "recommends the optimal method in 411/432 scenarios (95.0%)." But "optimal" is defined as the method producing the lowest residual bias in MC simulation. This is an oracle comparison -- in practice, the researcher does not know the true bias and must rely on estimated C. The framework's accuracy when C is estimated with noise from a finite validation sample (realistic setting) is not tested. If C estimation error degrades recommendation accuracy from 95% to 80%, the practical value is substantially reduced.
38	
39	W7. **[Minor] Theory-MC match rate discrepancy.** The paper summary in the task description states 33/36 match (3 mismatches), while the paper itself reports 35/36 (97.2%, 1 mismatch). The median-tier dream context also uses 33/36. This inconsistency should be resolved. If the actual match rate depends on the outcome tier (best vs. median imagined results), the 33/36 figure should be used as the more conservative estimate.
40	
41	W8. **[Minor] Only 11 references.** For a paper spanning measurement error theory, causal inference, NLP annotation, and sensitivity analysis, 11 references is unusually sparse. Notable omissions include: Carroll et al. (2006) textbook on measurement error in nonlinear models; Ogburn & VanderWeele (2012) on mediator misclassification; Angelopoulos et al. (2023) on prediction-powered inference (PPI is a core correction method); Imai & Yamamoto (2010) on sensitivity analysis for mediation; Veierod & Laake (2001) on polytomous exposure misclassification (listed in the project's own literature_map.yaml but not cited in the paper).
42	
43	### Questions for Authors
44	
45	Q1. **Non-differentiality robustness:** Have you tested what happens to the topology-dependent reversal dichotomy when C varies by 10-20% across strata of a conditioning variable? Specifically, if C is mildly differential (C_stratum1 and C_stratum2 differ by Frobenius distance 0.05-0.10), do the "information-loss" topologies remain at zero violations? This is critical for LLM applications where annotation difficulty varies with text features.
46	
47	Q2. **Dirichlet subspace for non-exposure topologies:** You report the Dirichlet subspace analysis (violation rates at diag>=0.5, 0.7, 0.9) only for exposure misclassification. What are the corresponding stratified violation rates for mediation, collider, and front-door? If mediation's violation rate at diag>=0.7 is, say, 2% rather than 52%, the practical risk profile changes dramatically.
48	
49	Q3. **ASV coverage reconciliation:** The task summary states 0.907 average coverage at 95% nominal, the paper reports 0.952, and the held-out empirical figure is 0.94. Can you clarify the source of each number and whether they correspond to different evaluation protocols?
50	
```

```text
50	
51	Q4. **Correction framework under estimated C:** The 95% accuracy is computed with the true C matrix known. What happens when C is estimated from a validation sample of size n_gold = 500, 1000, 2000? At what n_gold does recommendation accuracy degrade below 85%?
52	
53	Q5. **Ecological validity of topological contexts:** For the 9 empirical scenarios in Table 11 (ASV concordance), are the topological assignments (e.g., protest coding as mediator, toxicity as collider) drawn from actual published CSS research designs? If so, please cite the studies. If not, please acknowledge the limitation.
54	
55	Q6. **Binarization collapse -- choice of collapse mapping:** The binarization analysis collapses K categories to 2, but there are multiple possible collapse mappings (e.g., for K=5, there are 2^4 - 2 = 14 non-trivial binary partitions). Are the reported Delta values averaged over all possible partitions, or do they use a specific partition? The choice of collapse mapping could dominate the Delta magnitude.
56	
57	Q7. **Front-door topology -- practical relevance:** Of the six topologies, front-door is the least commonly used in CSS practice (it requires a specific intermediate variable satisfying the front-door criterion). Including it inflates the "4/6 topologies show reversal" count. How would the narrative change if front-door were excluded and the count became "3/5 common topologies"?
58	
59	### Minor Issues
60	
61	M1. Table 1 uses "Violation Rate (100k)" in column headers but the table body shows rates to 3 decimal places (0.000, 0.850, etc.) -- these are proportions, not counts. Clarify units.
62	
63	M2. The ASV definition uses ||C - C_0||_F <= g(delta) but g(delta) is not defined explicitly. What functional form does g take? Is it linear, quadratic, or topology-specific?
64	
65	M3. The "information-loss vs. structural-distortion" dichotomy is a post-hoc categorization of the six topologies. The paper should discuss whether this dichotomy has a formal characterization (e.g., based on d-separation properties under misclassification) or is purely descriptive.
66	
67	M4. Table 3 (violation rate scaling with K) reports only confounding, mediation, and collider. The other four topologies (exposure, M-bias, instrument, front-door) are omitted. Since exposure is the strongest reversal case, its K=5,7 scaling should be reported.
68	
69	M5. The correction framework recommends "naive plug-in" when ASV > 5.0, described as cases where correction is unnecessary. This should be labeled more precisely -- it is a "no correction needed" recommendation, not a "correction method."
70	
71	M6. Section 7 (Structural Diagnostics) reports correlations (r = -0.91 for spectral gap) across what appears to be all topologies pooled. These correlations should be reported per-topology, as the relationship between spectral gap and bias magnitude is likely topology-dependent (trivially r=0 for confounding/instrument/M-bias where violation rate is 0).
72	
73	M7. The bibliography entries use inconsistent formats. Several entries lack DOIs, page numbers, or complete author lists (abbreviated as "et al." in the bib itself, which is unusual for a .bib file). Baumann et al. (2025) is listed as ACL 2025 in the bib but as "arXiv" in the literature_map -- verify the correct venue.
74	
75	### Missing Experiments / Gaps vs Design
76	
77	Comparing the paper content against the 10-experiment design:
78	
79	1. **plan_001 (K=3 bias expressions + MC):** Fully covered. 35/36 match reported (note: task summary says 33/36 -- discrepancy).
80	
81	2. **plan_002 (General-K matrix expressions):** Covered. Tables 3-4 report K=5,7 scaling and eigendecomposition. However, only 3 topologies (confounding, mediation, collider) are shown for K=5,7 -- the remaining 4 topologies' K=5,7 results are absent. The experiment plan says "Validate on confounding + mediation + collider DAGs with K=5,7" so this is as designed, but the paper then claims general applicability.
82	
83	3. **plan_003 (Empirical confusion matrices):** Covered. ~270K annotations, 9 C matrices reported. Missing: exact prompt templates, gold-standard procurement methodology, dataset preprocessing details, specific model versions/API endpoints.
84	
85	4. **plan_004 (ASV formal proofs):** Covered in structure (Theorems 1-2) but **no proof appendix is provided**. The proof technique descriptions are given but the actual proofs are absent. For a paper claiming formal proofs as a core contribution, this is a significant gap. The experiment plan specified "formal proofs of (a) monotonicity, (b) sharpness" -- stating proof techniques is not the same as providing proofs.
86	
87	5. **plan_005 (ASV MC validation, remaining topologies):** Fully covered. Table 7, zero monotonicity violations, sharpness gaps <0.008.
88	
89	6. **plan_006 (ASV empirical calibration):** Covered. 8/9 concordance, held-out coverage 0.94. Missing: the planned "ASV interpretability rating" pilot study (N=5 researchers, 4/5 preference) is mentioned in the dream data but not in the paper.
90	
91	7. **plan_007 (Correction guidance framework):** Fully covered. 432 scenarios, 95% accuracy, transparent failure analysis.
92	
93	8. **plan_008 (Binarization collapse):** Fully covered. Table 5, max Delta=0.19, 12% direction reversal at K=5,7.
94	
95	9. **plan_009 (Pathological C analysis):** Covered. Table 15, spectral gap diagnostics. However, the "practitioner risk map" visualization (planned Fig 20) is absent -- this was identified as one of the most actionable outputs for practitioners.
96	
97	10. **plan_010 (annot-sensitivity toolkit):** Described in Section 8 but not verified. No link to code repository, no test results shown, no benchmark timing reported in the paper (the plan specifies <0.1s for bias_expression, <1s for asv_bound). Toolkit existence is claimed but not demonstrated.
98	
99	**Critical gap not in the 10-experiment plan:** No experiment tests robustness to violations of the non-differential misclassification assumption. Given that this assumption is the foundation of all theoretical results, and that LLM errors are plausibly differential in practice, this is the single most important missing experiment.
100	
101	**Figures:** The paper contains zero figures despite the experiment plan specifying 20+ visualizations. The topology reversal heatmap (Fig 1), ASV sensitivity curves (Fig 11/15), and practitioner risk map (Fig 20) would substantially improve comprehension and persuasiveness. For a paper whose core results are inherently visual (topology-dependent patterns, continuous sensitivity curves, 2D risk maps), this is a major presentation deficit.
102	
103	### Score: 4/6
104	### Confidence: 4/5
105	
106	**Rationale:** The methodological contributions are genuine and the experimental execution is thorough across the 10-experiment plan. The core finding (topology-dependent reversal) is well-supported by both theory and MC simulation. However, three factors prevent a score of 5: (1) the untested non-differentiality assumption, which is central to practical applicability; (2) the absence of proof appendix for claimed formal proofs; and (3) the zero-figure presentation. The paper is above the acceptance threshold but needs revisions to reach its potential. With the non-differentiality robustness experiment, a proof appendix, and 3-4 key figures added, this would be a clear accept.
```

(2025) is listed as ACL 2025 in the bib but as \"arXiv\" in the literature_map -- verify the correct venue.\n\n### Missing Experiments / Gaps vs Design\n\nComparing the paper content against the 10-experiment design:\n\n1. **plan_001 (K=3 bias expressions + MC):** Fully covered. 35/36 match reported (note: task summary says 33/36 -- discrepancy).\n\n2. **plan_002 (General-K matrix expressions):** Covered. Tables 3-4 report K=5,7 scaling and eigendecomposition. However, only 3 topologies (confounding, mediation, collider) are shown for K=5,7 -- the remaining 4 topologies' K=5,7 results are absent. The experiment plan says \"Validate on confounding + mediation + collider DAGs with K=5,7\" so this is as designed, but the paper then claims general applicability.\n\n3. **plan_003 (Empirical confusion matrices):** Covered. ~270K annotations, 9 C matrices reported. Missing: exact prompt templates, gold-standard procurement methodology, dataset preprocessing details, specific model versions/API endpoints.\n\n4. **plan_004 (ASV formal proofs):** Covered in structure (Theorems 1-2) but **no proof appendix is provided**. The proof technique descriptions are given but the actual proofs are absent. For a paper claiming formal proofs as a core contribution, this is a significant gap. The experiment plan specified \"formal proofs of (a) monotonicity, (b) sharpness\" -- stating proof techniques is not the same as providing proofs.\n\n5. **plan_005 (ASV MC validation, remaining topologies):** Fully covered. Table 7, zero monotonicity violations, sharpness gaps <0.008.\n\n6. **plan_006 (ASV empirical calibration):** Covered. 8/9 concordance, held-out coverage 0.94. Missing: the planned \"ASV interpretability rating\" pilot study (N=5 researchers, 4/5 preference) is mentioned in the dream data but not in the paper.\n\n7. **plan_007 (Correction guidance framework):** Fully covered. 432 scenarios, 95% accuracy, transparent failure analysis.\n\n8. **plan_008 (Binarization collapse):** Fully covered. Table 5, max Delta=0.19, 12% direction reversal at K=5,7.\n\n9. **plan_009 (Pathological C analysis):** Covered. Table 15, spectral gap diagnostics. However, the \"practitioner risk map\" visualization (planned Fig 20) is absent -- this was identified as one of the most actionable outputs for practitioners.\n\n10. **plan_010 (annot-sensitivity toolkit):** Described in Section 8 but not verified. No link to code repository, no test results shown, no benchmark timing reported in the paper (the plan specifies <0.1s for bias_expression, <1s for asv_bound). Toolkit existence is claimed but not demonstrated.\n\n**Critical gap not in the 10-experiment plan:** No experiment tests robustness to violations of the non-differential misclassification assumption. Given that this assumption is the foundation of all theoretical results, and that LLM errors are plausibly differential in practice, this is the single most important missing experiment.\n\n**Figures:** The paper contains zero figures despite the experiment plan specifying 20+ visualizations. The topology reversal heatmap (Fig 1), ASV sensitivity curves (Fig 11/15), and practitioner risk map (Fig 20) would substantially improve comprehension and persuasiveness. For a paper whose core results are inherently visual (topology-dependent patterns, continuous sensitivity curves, 2D risk maps), this is a major presentation deficit.\n\n### Score: 4/6\n### Confidence: 4/5\n\n**Rationale:** The methodological contributions are genuine and the experimental execution is thorough across the 10-experiment plan. The core finding (topology-dependent reversal) is well-supported by both theory and MC simulation. However, three factors prevent a score of 5: (1) the untested non-differentiality assumption, which is central to practical applicability; (2) the absence of proof appendix for claimed formal proofs; and (3) the zero-figure presentation. The paper is above the acceptance threshold but needs revisions to reach its potential. With the non-differentiality robustness experiment, a proof appendix, and 3-4 key figures added, this would be a clear accept.",

_source line 3326 · 2026-05-21T05:14:15.025Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KRutHmchqdth92842UfcAC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic characterization of how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology. Our bias taxonomy across seven topologies reveals that the classical binary guarantee of bias toward the null breaks for $K \geq 3$: exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal (83.4\% and 52.6\% respectively), while five topologies maintain a structural guarantee of no sign flip. The ASV framework provides topology-aware sensitivity diagnostics, revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune). Our correction guidance analysis reveals that correction method effectiveness is itself topology-dependent: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves matrix-based corrections are ineffective for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction at the cost of gold-standard labels. Binarization collapse analysis further demonstrates that simplifying multi-class annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies.
5	
6	\paragraph{Limitations.}
7	Our framework assumes non-differential misclassification: $P(A^*|A, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*|A)$. This holds when the LLM annotator has no access to the outcome $Y$, but may be violated when few-shot examples are selected conditional on $Y$ or when annotation difficulty varies with the outcome. Under differential error, the bias expressions no longer hold and the ASV framework provides an upper bound rather than an exact characterization. Closed-form bias expressions are derived for $K{=}3$ and verified numerically at $K{=}5,7$; expressions for arbitrary $K$ remain open. The framework assumes a single annotator; extending to correlated multi-annotator settings~\cite{kim_garg2025} is an open problem. The empirical validation spans $K{=}2,3,10$ with four LLMs, confirming the theoretical bias ranking, sign-flip guarantees, and $F_1$ disconnect across all $K$; however, VAST and CivilComments use a 7B model rather than frontier models, limiting direct cross-tier comparisons.
8	
9	\paragraph{Future work.}
10	Immediate directions include extending to differential misclassification and incorporating correlated multi-LLM error structures into correction strategies. We release an open-source Python toolkit (\texttt{annot-sensitivity}) that computes ASVs from estimated confusion matrices and routes to topology-appropriate correction methods.
11	
```

```text
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone. The taxonomy extends to binarization: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying up to 147$\times$ across topologies.
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a negative result with significant practical implications that, while theoretically predicted by Frisch--Waugh--Lovell, has been overlooked in the annotation correction literature: matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies, because the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels.
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

```text
Excellent! The IV Dirichlet subspace results are very interesting:

| Diagonal Threshold | Violation | Sign Flip | Amplification | Worst Severity |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ≥0.0 (unrestricted) | 100% | 51.7% | 48.3% | 106,206× |
| ≥0.5 | 100% | 0% | 100% | 28.6× |


| ≥0.7 | 100% | 0% | 100% | 2.37× |
| ≥0.9 | 100% | 0% | 100% | 1.24× |

The pattern here is striking: IV sign reversals completely disappear once I enforce a diagonal threshold of 0.5 or higher, whereas exposure still shows sign reversals even at 0.7. What's remarkable is that IV maintains universal violation and amplification across all accuracy levels, but the worst-case severity plummets dramatically as the threshold tightens—from over 106,000× down to just 1.24×.

The key insight is that IV's sign reversal is entirely a product of near-random confusion matrices, so at realistic LLM accuracy levels it manifests only as amplification, never reversal. The amplification persists stubbornly though, reaching 2.37× even at diag≥0.7. This contrasts sharply with exposure, which shows more sporadic violations but maintains sign reversal longer. IV is uniformly vulnerable across all accuracies but lacks the sign reversal mechanism that exposure retains, making this a compelling distinction for the paper.
```

1	% =====================================================================
2	% IV topology Dirichlet subspace analysis
3	% Source: artifacts/mvp_iv_dirichlet_subspace.py
4	% Seed: 42, N=100,000 per threshold, K=3
5	% =====================================================================
6	
7	\subsection{IV Wald Estimator: Diagonal-Dominance Subspaces}
8	\label{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}
9	
10	Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy_results} reports a 52.6\% Wald sign-flip rate
11	over the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior. Because realistic LLM confusion
12	matrices are diagonal-dominant, we replicate the exposure-DAG stratification
13	of \S\ref{sec:taxonomy_results} for the IV topology, restricting the sample
14	space to $\{C : \min_k C_{kk} \geq d\}$ for $d \in \{0, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9\}$.
15	Sampling uses truncated Beta$(1,K{-}1)$ for each diagonal entry and a
16	uniform split of the residual mass across off-diagonals, equivalent to
17	Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ per column conditioned on the diagonal constraint.
18	
19	The Wald probability limit under misclassification factors as
20	\[
21	\hat\tau_{\text{Wald}}(C)
22	   \;=\; \frac{\mathrm{Cov}(Z,Y)/\mathrm{Var}(Z)}
23	              {\bigl(C\,\mathrm{FS}\bigr)_{1}},
24	\qquad
25	\mathrm{FS}_k \;=\; \frac{\mathrm{Cov}(Z, D_k)}{\mathrm{Var}(Z)},
26	\]
27	where the reduced form ($\mathrm{RF}$) and first-stage vector
28	$\mathrm{FS}$ are estimated from a 2{,}000{,}000-sample precomputation of
29	\texttt{gen\_iv} with the default IV parameterization
30	($\mathrm{d}_Z{=}(0,1,{-}0.5)$, $\mathrm{d}_U{=}(0,0.8,0.6)$,
31	$\beta{=}(0,1,{-}0.5)$, $\lambda{=}1$); numerically,
32	$\mathrm{RF}{=}0.3040$, $\mathrm{FS}{=}({-}0.0382,\,0.2154,\,{-}0.1772)$,
33	$\tau_{\text{true}}{=}\mathrm{RF}/\mathrm{FS}_1{=}1.4114$.
34	
35	\paragraph{Subspace results.}
36	Table~\ref{tab:iv_dirichlet_subspace} reports the violation, sign-flip,
37	amplification, and worst-case severity over 100{,}000 random matrices per
38	threshold. The full-space sign-flip rate replicates the 52.6\% headline
39	within Monte-Carlo error (51.7\%). Critically, diagonal-dominance \emph{does
40	not protect} the Wald estimator: the violation rate is 100\% at every
41	$d \geq 0.5$, and the only effect of increasing $d$ is to convert sign
42	flips into same-sign amplification.
43	
44	\begin{table}[t]
45	\centering
46	\caption{IV Wald estimator bias by minimum-diagonal subspace.
47	Violation: $|\hat\tau| > |\tau|$.
48	Sign flip: $\mathrm{sign}(\hat\tau) \neq \mathrm{sign}(\tau)$.
49	Amplification: $|\hat\tau| > |\tau|$ with same sign (disjoint from sign flip).
50	Worst severity: $\max |\hat\tau / \tau|$ conditional on violation.
51	Rates over 100{,}000 Dirichlet-truncated random $C$ per threshold,
52	$K{=}3$, $\tau_{\text{true}}{=}1.411$.}
53	\label{tab:iv_dirichlet_subspace}
54	\small
55	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
56	\toprule
57	\textbf{Subspace} & \textbf{Violation} & \textbf{Sign Flip} & \textbf{Amplif.} & \textbf{Worst} \\
58	\midrule
59	Full           & 100.0\% & 51.7\% & 48.3\% & $10^{5}\!\times$ \\
60	$d \geq 0.5$   & 100.0\% & 0.0\%  & 100.0\% & $28.6\times$ \\
61	$d \geq 0.7$   & 100.0\% & 0.0\%  & 100.0\% & $2.4\times$ \\
62	$d \geq 0.9$   & 100.0\% & 0.0\%  & 100.0\% & $1.2\times$ \\
63	\bottomrule
64	\end{tabular}
65	\end{table}
66	
67	\paragraph{Mechanism.}
68	For the calibrated DGP, $\mathrm{FS}_1$ is the unique maximizer of
69	$\mathrm{FS}$ over $k$. Any column-stochastic $C$ with $C_{11}<1$ replaces
70	some of the first-stage signal on category~1 with a convex combination of
71	the weaker (and oppositely-signed) signals on $k\in\{0,2\}$, so
72	$(C\,\mathrm{FS})_1 < \mathrm{FS}_1$ almost surely. Because
73	$\mathrm{RF}$ is invariant to $C$, the Wald ratio inflates monotonically as
74	the denominator contracts. Sign flips occur precisely when the contraction
75	overshoots zero. Diagonal-dominance bounds how close $(C\,\mathrm{FS})_1$
76	can approach zero but does not bound it away from $\mathrm{FS}_1$;
77	consequently amplification is unavoidable while sign reversal becomes
78	infeasible for sufficiently large $d$. Numerically, the boundary sits
79	near $d{\approx}0.5$: at this threshold, sign reversal has already
80	disappeared but worst-case amplification still reaches $28.6\times$.
81	
82	\paragraph{Implications.}
83	The qualitative diagonal-dominance argument that rescues the exposure DAG
84	in the $d{\geq}0.7$ subspace (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}) does not
85	generalize to IV. Even at $d{\geq}0.9$---a high-accuracy regime that few
86	LLM classifiers attain on three-way tasks---the Wald estimator amplifies
87	$|\tau|$ with probability $\approx 1$, with a worst-case inflation of
88	$1.24\times$. Researchers running Wald-style IV with annotated treatments
89	should therefore (i) report the empirical $\min_k C_{kk}$ of their
90	classifier, and (ii) treat any reported point estimate as an upper bound on
91	$|\tau|$ unless they explicitly correct for misclassification.
92

578	The sign-alignment condition is violated when the confounder has opposing effects on treatment and outcome (e.g., $\gamma_A > 0$ on $T$ but $\gamma_A < 0$ on $Y$), producing $\operatorname{sign}(\tau) \neq \operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{OV}})$. In applied settings, such opposing effects are uncommon when the analyst adjusts for $A$ precisely because they believe it confounds the $T$--$Y$ relationship in a consistent direction. When the condition fails, sign reversal becomes possible under sufficient misclassification, though this represents a pathological DGP where adjustment itself may be questionable.
579

Because realistic LLM confusion\nmatrices are diagonal-dominant, we replicate the exposure-DAG stratification\nfor the IV topology, restricting the sample\nspace to $\\{C : \\min_k C_{kk} \\geq d\\}$ for $d \\in \\{0, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9\\}$\n(100{,}000 random matrices per threshold).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{IV Wald estimator bias by minimum-diagonal subspace ($K{=}3$, $\\tau_{\\text{true}}{=}1.41$).\nRates over 100{,}000 Dirichlet-truncated random $C$ per threshold.}\n\\label{tab:iv_dirichlet_subspace}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Subspace} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & \\textbf{Worst} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull           & 100.0\\% & 51.7\\% & 48.3\\% & $10^{5}\\!\\times$ \\\\\n$d \\geq 0.5$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $28.6\\times$ \\\\\n$d \\geq 0.7$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $2.4\\times$ \\\\\n$d \\geq 0.9$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $1.2\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe contrast with exposure is instructive: at $d{\\geq}0.7$, exposure violation drops to 13.7\\% (sign reversal 2.1\\%, amplification 11.7\\%), while IV maintains 100\\% violation with pure amplification. IV sign reversal is entirely driven by pathological (near-random) confusion matrices where the Wald denominator crosses zero; diagonal dominance prevents this crossing but cannot prevent amplification. Even at $d{\\geq}0.9$, the Wald estimator inflates $|\\tau|$ by up to $1.24\\times$ with probability~1."
}
```

### Tool result

_source line 3415 · 2026-05-21T05:17:59.057Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016UfapT4iJYxUSsRGQwKHFA`</summary>

```text
26	
27	\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}
28	The instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact.
29	
30	\paragraph{Cross-$K$ robustness.}
31	Experiments at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$ (100{,}000 random matrices per topology) confirm that all structural patterns from $K{=}3$ persist. The sign-flip guarantee holds for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\%. Worst-case bias magnitudes decrease modestly with $K$ for most topologies but increase for IV at low accuracy (worst case 14.0$\times$ at $K{=}3$ diagonal $\geq 0.5$).
```

</details>

Diagonal-dominance subspace analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}) reveals that IV sign reversal vanishes at diagonal $\\geq 0.5$, but unlike exposure, the violation rate remains at 100\\% (pure amplification) even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$.",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 25,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 25,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Five of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Note that this zero sign-flip result for confounding is consistent with \\citet{brenner1993} and \\citet{dosemeci1990}, who showed that polytomous misclassification can bias \\emph{away} from the null: their estimand compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the truth, whereas our sign-flip metric compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the unadjusted (omitted-variable) estimate. Amplification without sign reversal is expected under both definitions. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\\% violation, 10.4\\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}",
          "-The instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact.",
          "+The instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact. Diagonal-dominance subspace analysis (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}) reveals that IV sign reversal vanishes at diagonal $\\geq 0.5$, but unlike exposure, the violation rate remains at 100\\% (pure amplification) even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Cross-$K$ robustness.}",
          " Experiments at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$ (100{,}000 random matrices per topology) confirm that all structural patterns from $K{=}3$ persist. The sign-flip guarantee holds for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\\%. Worst-case bias magnitudes decrease modestly with $K$ for most topologies but increase for IV at low accuracy (worst case 14.0$\\times$ at $K{=}3$ diagonal $\\geq 0.5$)."
        ]
      }

2	  "raw": "Results delivered.\n\n**Headline numbers** (K=3, 100k samples/threshold, seed 42):\n\n| Subspace | Violation | Sign-Flip | Amplification | Worst |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Full | 100.0% | **51.7%** | 48.3% | 10⁵× |\n| d≥0.5 | 100.0% | 0.0% | 100.0% | 28.6× |\n| d≥0.7 | 100.0% | 0.0% | 100.0% | 2.4× |\n| d≥0.9 | 100.0% | 0.0% | 100.0% | 1.2× |\n\nFull-space sign-flip 51.7% matches the paper's 52.6% within MC error (different precompute seed; well inside the 2% tolerance).\n\n**Key qualitative finding**: diagonal-dominance does **not** rescue IV the way it rescues exposure. Because FS₁ is the unique max of FS, (C·FS)₁ < FS₁ almost surely for any C ≠ I, so the Wald denominator monotonically contracts → 100% amplification at every threshold. Increasing d only converts sign flips into same-sign amplification; it never restores correctness. Even at d≥0.9 every Wald estimate inflates |τ|.\n\n**Artifacts written**:\n- `artifacts/mvp_iv_dirichlet_subspace.py` — self-contained script\n- `artifacts/mvp_iv_dirichlet_subspace_output.log` — console output\n- `artifacts/iv_dirichlet_subspace_results.json` — structured results\n- `artifacts/iv_dirichlet_subspace_appendix.tex` — appendix paragraph + table\n\n(Ran locally rather than on home-1x5090: the project's `annot_sensitivity/` module lives only on the local box, and the analysis is pure-numpy ~1.3s — no GPU needed. JSON path matches the spec.)"
3	}

Because FS₁ is the unique max of FS, (C·FS)₁ < FS₁ almost surely for any C ≠ I, so the Wald denominator monotonically contracts → 100% amplification at every threshold. Increasing d only converts sign flips into same-sign amplification; it never restores correctness. Even at d≥0.9 every Wald estimate inflates |τ|.\\n\\n**Artifacts written**:\\n- `artifacts/mvp_iv_dirichlet_subspace.py` — self-contained script\\n- `artifacts/mvp_iv_dirichlet_subspace_output.log` — console output\\n- `artifacts/iv_dirichlet_subspace_results.json` — structured results\\n- `artifacts/iv_dirichlet_subspace_appendix.tex` — appendix paragraph + table\\n\\n(Ran locally rather than on home-1x5090: the project's `annot_sensitivity/` module lives only on the local box, and the analysis is pure-numpy ~1.3s — no GPU needed. JSON path matches the spec.)\"\n}",
      "numLines": 3,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 3
    }

{
  "project": "causal_annot_error_attrib",
  "message": "之前的指令你可能卡住了。IV Dirichlet subspace 已经完成（我已整合到 appendix 并 commit）。\n\n现在只需要做一件事：**Non-differential misclassification stress test**\n\n设计一个简单的 simulation：在 exposure topology 下，引入轻微的 differential error。\n\n**具体做法**：\n1. 生成 random C_base（Dirichlet(1,1,1)，K=3）\n2. 对于每个 ε ∈ {0, 0.01, 0.05, 0.1}：\n   - C_treated = normalize_columns(C_base + ε·Δ)\n   - C_control = normalize_columns(C_base - ε·Δ)  \n   - 其中 Δ 是随机扰动矩阵\n   - 在 exposure topology 下用 C_treated 处理 treatment group，C_control 处理 control group\n   - 计算 sign-flip rate\n3. 10,000 random C per ε level（不需要 100k，这是 robustness check）\n4. 报告 sign-flip rate vs ε\n\n**直接本地跑就行**，不需要 SSH 到远程。纯 numpy，几秒钟就能跑完。\n\n产出：artifacts/nondiff_stress_test_results.json + artifacts/nondiff_stress_test_appendix.tex"
}
